[trialAccount t18330905-162]
Before Mr. Justice Patteson.
1365. WILLIAM THOMAS STONER was indicted, for that he on the 8th of July, with a certain gun, loaded with gunpowder and leaden shot, which he in both hands had, and held, feloniously, maliciously, and unlawfully, did shoot at William Upjohn, with intent, feloniously, wilfully, and of his malice aforethought, to kill and murder him against the statute, &c.
3 OTHER COUNTS stating his intent to be to maim, disable, or to do him some grievous bodily harm.
ROBERT JONES. I live with my mother, at Ranelagh-place. On the 8th of July, I was in company with William Upjohn, in Mr. Cubitt's fields, near the Willow-walk, over the Grosvenor Canal - we were throwing at some frogs, in the ditch, the field is Mr. Cubitt's, and on the other side of the ditch is a garden; we were throwing the stones into the ditch - while we were throwing the stones some man came behind some trees and shot at William Upjohn - I saw the man, but could not see his face - I did not know who it was at that time; when he fired, he said, "Now I have got you;" I did not see his face at any time - I now know who it was, because I saw him taken to the station-house - I did not see his face before that; Upjohn's back was towards him - I took him to the doctor's, in Vauxhall-road, and afterwards he was taken to St. George's Hospital - I did not see how the man was dressed at all.
WILLIAM UPJOHN. I was with Jones throwing stones, on the 8th of July - I heard a gun go off - I was hit at the back of the head - I turned round and saw who fired at me - I thought it was a man in a velvet coat, it appeared like a velvet coat; I saw the man's face, but the prisoner is the man I am sure, I saw his face, I did not hear him say anything for I was stunned; I heard the gun but nothing else; I was stunned immediately the shot hit me; I took and ran a little way and then fell; I did not look round as I was running; I turned round directly I was shot, I thought it was a stone hit me, and when I turned round I saw the man, I am sure it was the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Do you think you were so much stunned, that if a man called out"Halloo, I have caught you," you could not have heard it? A. Yes, I could hear nothing; being stunned did not hurt my sight as well as my hearing; there was a hedge between me and the man, and a ditch, it was a very high hedge and pretty thick; the man was in the garden and I in the field - the trees were in leaf.
COURT. Q. How high was the hedge, could you see over it? A. Yes; we were down in a hollow, the hedge went up high - we could see right into the garden; I saw the man's face, I saw him come from the tree two or three steps, his face was above the hedge - I could see him quite plain, I did not see him till after the shot was fired.
HENRY CHARLES JOHNSON. I am house-surgeon of St. George's Hospital. Upjohn was brought to me on the 8th of July, I examined him, he had about fifteen small wounds on the left-side of the head, in the back part - I did not take any shot out of his head - I fancied I felt shot there but none were discovered - there is no other surgeon here; he had been to another surgeon before - he was discharged on the 24th of July; the wounds were such as would be caused by the shot of a gun.
Cross-examined. Q. Would not coarse powder make such wounds? A. If the gun was near enough, but not that kind of wound - I examined the wound to see if I could discover any shot, but found none.
GEORGE THATCHER (policeman). I went with Mrs. Upjohn, the boy's mother, to the prisoner's employer on Wednesday, the 8th of July. I saw the prisoner there, and asked him where the gun was which he shot the boy with; he told me it was in the yard, at the back of the house - I asked him if he would let me have it, he was going back to fetch it, when one of the servants brought it to me; he said, he shot at the boys, but did not mean to shoot them - he also stated that the boys were throwing bricks into the ditch, to get over into the garden to steal the fruit - he said, he took the gun up, but he meant to shoot over their heads; he did not tell me how it happened that he shot them.
Cross-examined. Q. He told you he did not mean to hit the boys? A. He said he took the gun up and shot at them, but meant to shoot over their heads - I have seen the place many times - there is a hedge, but it lies in a hollow, and the ditch lies in a hollow also - the boys are very low in stature.
ROBERT JONES re-examined. The man who fired the gun was about ten yards from us. I was at the doctor's with Upjohn, the doctor brought a light and examined him, and said, he was to be taken to the hospital immediately - he did not take anything out of his head, he felt his head, nothing more.
Cross-examined. Q. Was there any fruit in the garden? A. Yes, I knew that very well - I had never been there before; I cannot tell whether Upjohn had - I have not been into any garden since; I do not know Mr. Martin- I have been at liberty ever since; I was never taken on any charge of stealing fruit.
Prisoner's Defence (written). Gentlemen; - I most solemnly assure you, that when I saw the boys bringing the bricks from the kiln for the purpose of making a bridge over the ditch,(and by that means get into the garden to commit their depredations and plunder,) I was standing at the hovel which is in the garden, and as soon as I saw the boys I called out to them. I asked what they were doing there, and told them to be off. I left the hovel and approached towards the ditch; as I walked along, my feet got entangled amongst some potatoe haulms - I stumbled: I had the gun in my hand; it was loaded with only half a charge of powder, and a few sparrow shot. The gun was full cocked; in stumbling, I must have jerked the gun, which went off. I saw the boys run away (three one way and two another); I was quite unconscious of having done any injury, nor could I have supposed it possible from the situation in which I stood; and when it is considered that the hedge which is on the garden side of the ditch is one foot and a half through, and five feet high, closely set with poplar and elder trees, it would appear impossible to be otherwise than of a purely accidental nature, and the charge must have passed through the hedge and hit the prosecutor without my knowledge. I did not hear any person call out, and concluded that the boys had been frightened away. I returned back to the hovel, and remained on duty my usual time.
WILLIAM UPJOHN re-examined. I felt a probe going into my head, but don't know whetherany thing was taken out.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18330905-163]
Fifth Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
1366. FREDERICK SEYFFERT was indicted for, that he, on the 13th of July, in and upon John Benton, feloniously, unlawfully, and maliciously did make an assault, and with a certain sharp instrument, feloniously, &c. did him strike, stab, and cut him, in and upon the left side of his body, with intent, feloniously, wilfully, and of his malice aforethought, to kill and murder him, against the statute.
2 OTHER COUNTS stating his intent to be to disable him, and to do him some grievous bodily harm.
CHARLES SEYFFERT. I live at No. 52, Westbourne-street, Pimlico. I am a collector of the post-horse duty; the prisoner is my own son - he came to me on Saturday, the 13th of July, to the gate in the King's road, where I take the tickets for the post-horse duty; he was there the whole day - he came about five o'clock in the morning, and had breakfast and dinner, and afterwards got very wild, and made use of expressions towards his mother and me, threatening our lives - he had taken no spirits to my knowledge - he was with me the whole day; he was abusing us all day long - several times in the course of the day he put himself in a position to destroy hismother and me with a knife and fork - he d-d his mother, and called her a w-e, b-h, and sow, and said he would rip her bl-y guts out, and made an attempt at it with the knife - I called a constable, but he said he could not act unless he saw a blow struck.
Q. At the time you applied to the constable, had the prisoner any weapon in his hand? A. Yes, a fork; the policeman saw it in his hand, and he told him if he came near him he would stick him, and that made him afraid to touch him; it went on till a quarter before nine o'clock in the evening, and then I spoke to sergeant Benton, and told him how my son was - he was raving mad; he has been in several asylums; he was at the Hanwell nineteen weeks; and at Tottenham eight weeks; he cut his throat there; and was strapped down at Nottingham eight weeks; and about a fortnight before this happened, he was in Tothill-fields prison for attempting to drown himself - he struck the park-keeper because he would not let him go into the park.
Q. Well, you spoke to sergeant Benton - was the prisoner at that time making use of threats to you? A. Yes; he shut the half-hatch - I had got his mother away from him; Benton was coming to relieve, and the prisoner told me if I came in he would rip my b - y guts out; there was a stone on the board, and he sharpened a three-pronged fork - that was between eight and nine o'clock; I told Benton he was raving mad, and I wished him taken to a place of safety; and the moment Benton came up, the prisoner stabbed him in his left side with the fork - he left the fork in his side until Benton pulled it out; I saw it sticking in his side- Benton drew it out, and then seized the prisoner and took him into custody - he was most decidedly mad at the time - he has been subject to fits of insanity for five or six years; when he is quiet he is very well; he acted the same to other people as towards me - whenever he was without restraint, he is very violent - no offence had been given him at all; he said I had sold him for £800 and bought a whole row of houses with the money; I believe he is at this time sane - he is very rational at times, but goes off all of a minute; when he is right in his mind, he behaves very well - he is always fancying there is something after him when in his right senses; he is wavering, fancying he sees birds or something coming after him.
Prisoner. I consider it impossible for him to see at the distance he stood off, whether anything was done at all; for fourteen policemen rushed on me directly - Benton struck me with a stick. Witness. I told Benton he could not do it by himself, and about three policemen came with him, but we were all within a yard of the door; nobody struck the prisoner - Benton would have disarmed him, but while his arm was up, he stuck him in the side.
Prisoner. He may say what he likes; he has been trying to put me away; he has tried to poison me; here is a man here who was rendering assistance, when I was taken to Hanwell on the 17th of April 1832, and kept there until August, 1833; Mr. Meycock helped to put the straight waistcoat on me, all he wishes is to have me confined; I would sooner have my life taken away, than be confined at all; I put Benton on his guard before he came up to me - every thing he has spoken is quite fabric.
JOHN BENTON. (Policeman B 10.) On the evening of the 13th of July, I was spoken to by Seyffert, at the collecting house, about ten minutes after nine o'clock; I went to the collecting house, the prisoner was standing in the toll-house, with this fork in his hand; and directly I made my appearance before the door, he came forward, and thrust the fork an inch and an half into my left side; I was not at all acquainted with his previous habits; I lost a great deal of blood; he came at me again directly, and I thrust at him, and secured him, and pulled him over the half-door; I took the fork out of my side, and gave him to one of my men to take to the station-house; I went to St. George's Hospital; he thrust the fork into my side, as hard as he could; I had my great coat on, and it came sideways through my great coat, or I should have sustained much more injury; he did it with all his strength; I have no doubt he intended to take my life if he could; I had not spoken to him; he had heard his father desire me to come to his assistance.
Prisoner Q. When you came up to me, did you ask me to come with you? A. No; I never spoke to you.
Prisoner. I said "No, I have not offened you, nor any of your men," you immediately took up your truncheon, and made a blow at me. Witness. When he was coming forward to stab me with the fork I had my truncheon drawn, and was going to strike his arm down, but somebody caught hold of my arm, and my truncheon fell down; I had not raised my arm to strike, until I observed his motion with the fork, and he had made a blow at me; it was in self-defence I raised my staff; he was making a thrust at me with the fork.
FRANCIS HICKS. I am a surgeon at St. George's Hospital. On Saturday the 13th of July, I was there and examined Benton, I found three small punctured wounds, about an inch and a half deep, between the fifth and sixth ribs; it appeared to be done by a fork like the one produced; it was not a dangerous wound, although such wounds are sometimes dangerous; it depends on the state of the body; I should think if he had not been protected by a great coat, and it had penetrated half an inch deeper, the lungs would have been wounded, which would have made it a much more dangerous case; I think a great coat likely to prevent that; it was between the ribs; I could pass a probe down across the wound; I dressed it and attended him about five days till all danger was over, and then he was attended by the surgeon of the division; I did not think all danger was removed till five days after; I should call it a punctured wound; it was done by stabbing, there was no cutting at all; it was a mere puncture not an incision or cut.
Prisoner. I have nothing to say; I don't wish anybody called on my own behalf; he has tried to take my life several times.
JOSEPH MEACOCK. I have known the prisoner four or five years, he was in a very bad state of mind; at times quite deranged; he is always so when he is subject to violent passions, and takes dislikes, and uses violence; most chiefly to his father and mother, and his nearest friends; I know of no provocation which he received on these occasions; I believe him a deranged madman; a ragingmadman at times; he is quiet and peaceable at other times; it comes on suddenly without any notice; his father and mother can always discover something in his countenance when it is coming on, he is kind to his family when he is not so.
NOT GUILTY. Being Insane at the Time.
The prisoner is detained till His Majesty's pleasure be known.
[trialAccount t18330905-164]
1367. THOMAS CORMACK was indicted, for, that he on the 22nd of August, in and upon Ann Dargin, feloniously and unlawfully, and maliciously did make an assault, and with a certain sharp instrument feloniously, &c., did strike, cut, and wound her, in and upon her right arm, with intent to kill and murder her.
2 OTHER COUNTS, stating his intent to be to disable, and to do her some grievous bodily harm.
ANN DARGIN. I am a single woman and live at No. 8, Payne's-buildings, Whitecross-street. I have known the prisoner about three months; he sells matches; I never made any acquaintance with him; I work at pill box making; I recollect some months ago seeing him enter a house opposite to where I live; I saw him take Tommy Cremer 's bird off a nail near the window, it was in a cage; I did not see him carry it away; I heard next morning that the bird and cage were missing; about three weeks after that I saw the prisoner in Cow-heel-alley, and said to him, "Hallo where is Tommy Cremer's bird?" he made no reply but passed on; on Thursday the 22nd of August, I was in Cow-heel-alley, about four o'clock in the afternoon in company with John O'Hara; I put my hand on the prisoner's hat and asked him where was Tommy Cremer's bird; he immediately turned round, and cut me in the arm with a knife, which he had in his hand at the time; I had just put the flat of my hand on the top of his hat - it could not stop him at all; he turned sharp round after he had cut my arm with his knife, and laughed and said, "Ah, I have marked you;" it was cut as far as the bone in the fleshy part of the arm; it was cut to the bone, it gave me pain; I could not sleep for three nights with it; I was walking about all night with it; I had Dr. Leesom who the policeman took me to, he is not here, there is no surgeon here; I went home directly it was done, and shewed it to my mother, and she told me to go and look for a policeman which I did, and found Mr. Brooming in Blue Anchor-alley, and took him to Bell-alley, as the prisoner lodges there; a young person shewed me where he lodged, and there we found him; I gave him into custody- the wound was cut so as I could see the bone; I dare say it was half an inch deep; after he had done it he went away, as quick as he could; O'Hara did not go after him.
Prisoner. This person cannot let me alone; she has been constantly teasing me. Witness. I have not except what I have stated; I never spoke to him except on the two occasions, when I inquired for Tommy Cremer's bird; I never touched him but once on the hat as I passed by.
COURT. Q. You hit him? A. Yes, as he passed by; I just put the palm of my hand down on his hat lightly; I did not press my hand down; the hat did not fall over his eyes at all.
JURY. Q. Was he coming towards you or what? A. He was coming towards me; I saw the knife in his hand I was standing still, he came up towards me; I touched him on the back part of his hat just as he was passing by; before he had quite passed by, I gently put my hand on his hat.
COURT. Q. When did you say "Where is Tommy Cremer's bird?" did he stand still when you put your hand on his hat? A. Yes, he did; and then I asked him,"Where is Tommy Cremer 's bird?" he did not offer to strike me till I repeated that; I said it very quick, putting my hand on his hat, and saying it directly; I did not mean to affront him - I had seen him take the bird away, and I meant to tell him so, and as soon as I said that, he turned round and gave me the cut. NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18330905-165]
1368. JOSEPH ROBINSON was indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Frederick Arnott, on the 2nd of September, and stealing therein 50 yards of silk, value 7l. 10s., his property.
FREDERICK ARNOTT. I live in Satchwell's-rents, Bethnal-green, I am a musical-key maker. My wife takes in silk to weave for Mr. Mears, of Spitalfields - I left my house at half-past seven o'clock in the evening, my wife was in the house, and this silk was in the loom; I returned at half-past one o'clock in the morning; I was in Union-street, Hackney-road; and my wife came to me there about a quarter to nine o'clock, and we came home together at half-past one o'clock - I opened the door with a key, and the dog was inside, chained to the staple of the door; I had left him so - there was a light up stairs; and when I entered the shop, I saw a roll on the ground, and the silk gone off it - the back window was wide open - I gave information to a police officer - the premises were examined, and in consequence of information from my son I went to a marine store shop in Bethnal-green-road, and left my son there, in care while I got a policeman; and when I was within a short distance of the marine store shop, I saw the prisoner come from the corner of Waterloo-town, which leads from the shop; he went round another turning, and came up again, and then I saw him with the silk in his hand; he came out of a gateway; he had it tied up in a handkerchief, half out and half in; he saw me, and ran away, and chucked the silk down at my son's feet - I am confident he is the man - I picked the silk up, and gave it to the officer - the prisoner ran away; he was taken afterwards.
Cross-examined by MR. CHURCHILL. Q. Did you see it picked up? A. I took it up myself - when I left my house, I left my wife in it; I did not fasten it myself - it was five o'clock in the morning when I met the prisoner; it was not very light - I was in a flurry when I got my property, I was thankful to get it - I have seen him once or twice before, coming to whisper my boy away from his work; I am sure he is the man.
ELIZABETH ARNOTT. I am the prosecutor's wife. I left my house about half-past eight o'clock, and double locked the door, and took the key in my pocket; I bolted the outside shed-door, and the inside back-door top and bottom; the window was fastened down, and the shutter shut, and a lamp left burning on the chimney-piece - we got home about half-past one o'clock, found the window open, and missed the silk; the shutter was takendown, and laid on the wash-house tiles - my husband would have to make the property good; there was between fifty and sixty yards; I should have had to have paid 3s. a yard for it.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you leave anybody in the house? A. No; I had seen the prisoner once or twice; I don't know of his being acquainted with my son; he came once or twice, and called my son out; my son has gone and spoken to him at the door - my son went out that night about half-past six o'clock.
JOHN ARNOTT. I am the prosecutor's son. I came home at a quarter to two o'clock in the morning, and in consequence of what I heard, I went with my father to an old iron shop in Bethnal-green-road; he left me to watch there, and in about two or three minutes I saw the prisoner coming out of the gateway; I knew him; I was two or three yards from him; the instant he saw me he was very much confused, he looked very much flurried - I told him my mother had been robbed, and then he was still more confused; and then I told him I thought he must know about it, or he would not be confused; he made no answer; and a little time after he said, "If I had not wanted money I would not have done it;" I said I would give him all the money I had about me if he would bring it back, and I gave him 1s.; he said he knew I would tell, I said I would not - the policeman was then coming after him, and he ran away faster than I could run, and I lost him, and then I met him again, coming out of the gate with the work in his hand; I and my father pursued him, and he threw the silk down in Bethnal-green-road.
Cross-examined. Q. Had he not the silk the first time? A. No; I said he had better tell me all he knew about it, it would be better for him.
COURT. Q. Did you tell him that before he said he would not have taken it if he had not wanted money? A. He said that first of his own accord, and after that I said he had better tell me all about it; he made me no answer at first - he said he was now done; I am quite sure he said he was done, and should not have taken the things if he had not wanted money, before I promised him 1s. to bring it back, and to keep it secret.
MR. CHURCHILL. Q. Where were you all this evening? A. I had been after my father and mother, but could not find them; I walked about from eight till eleven o'clock - I had a pint of beer at a new beer shop - I went to Hackney-road, but they had left there, and gone somewhere else - I got home about eleven o'clock, but could not get in; and walked about again from one place to another, and got home at half-past two o'clock; I had been home three times before I could get in.
Q. What led you to suppose the silk would be at the marine store shop? A. I had heard the prisoner dealt with that man two months before - he has been in the room the silk was in - I had no hand in taking the silk - I had heard the prisoner had said he should not go home all night - I had met him that night, and he told me so.
THOMAS BUNCE (policeman H 138). The prosecutor applied to me early on the morning of the 3rd of September - I observed that the silk was cut away from the loom, the window open, and the shutter down - About five o'clock I was called to the neighbourhood of the marine store shop - the prosecutor and I went one way, and a few minutes afterwards I saw the prisoner and got the silk - I did not know him before; I am sure it was him I saw - he was taken in five minutes, about one hundred yards from the spot.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you go into the room? A. Yes, and examined the window, it appeared to have been opened by a square of glass being taken out, and undone inside.
JOSEPH SMITH (policeman). I stopped the prisoner about ten minutes or a quarter after five o'clock - when I first saw him he was running; when he saw me he stopped from running, and walked, and began to whistle a tune - I went up, seeing my brother officer running after him, and apprehended him at the corner of Waterloo-town; he said "Oh, there is nothing the matter; I have done nothing" - I said "I shall detain you till the party comes up" - Mr. Arnott came up, and charged him with the felony - I stopped him nearly a quarter of a mile from the marine store shop.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you search him? A. No; my brother officer did, and found one shilling on him, but no housebreaking implements.
JOHN ARNOTT. I gave him a shilling, when I asked him to bring the silk back.
Prisoner's Defence. I was in company with this young fellow all night, from eight o'clock till a quarter-past one - I was in the new beer shop where he was, till about ten minutes to eleven o'clock - I came out from there and left him, and was going home, and found the door shut - I came back to see if I could meet him; I met him at the Flower-pot, in Brick-lane; he told me to carry the bundle for him, and he would pay me in the morning - I carried it to the marine store shop, when he said he would meet me, and well pay me; and when he came to me in the morning he said "Joe, I am ruined; my father has found it out; I must go directly out of the country - if you know anything of it give me half the money, or if you will, go and get the bundle back" - I said I did not know where it was taken; I had taken it to where he told me, because I expeted him to pay me for my trouble - I went and got it, and he gave me the shilling; I went down the street, and saw the policeman, he told me to run, which I did, and was stopped; but I am quite innocent.
THOMAS BUNCE re-examined. The house appeared to me to be opened inside - a square or two was out of the sash - the shutter was inside, and had been opened from the inside, not outside; I consider a person inside must have done it.
NOT GUILTY.

1370. CAROLINE ANN BUCKMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of July, 1 £50 Bank note, themonies of Mary Harwood, in the dwelling-house of Deborah Turner , against the statute, &c.
MARY HARWOOD. I am single, and live with my sister Deborah Turner - she keeps a house in Chapel-street, Pentonville, in the parish of St. James' Clerkenwell - the prisoner had been her servant for twelve months, within a few days, before the 11th of July - on that day she was sent out to buy some pork; she did not return, and in the evening we heard she was in custody - my attention was then directed to a 50l. note, which was my property, and which I had had in a chest of drawers in my bed room; the drawer was locked, there was other cash in the same drawer; I did not miss the note till my attention was called to it - I had received it from Grote's, the bankers, on the 20th of June; I had only that one 50l. note - I saw the broker write a pencil memorandum of the number of that note, and the policeman has that memorandum - after I had received the note I compared the number and date of it with that memorandum, and I saw it was correct; it was No. 9139, dated 14th May, 1833 - it was my sole property, and that was the note I lost - I had not sent the prisoner to buy any linen, or to buy anything with that note.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Had your sister a part property in that note? A. No; nor in any of the money in that drawer - I don't know how the drawer had been opened; there was the appearance of the mahogany being chipped off a little, but I found it locked.
GEORGE READ. I keep a linendraper's shop, at No. 40, Upper-street, Islington - on the 11th of July the prisoner came to my shop, about eight o'clock in the evening; she said she wished to purchase a few goods, but she could not unless I could give her change for a 50l. note - I said I could give her change, but I asked where she came from; she said, from No. 14, Camden-street - I told her it was a larger amount than I liked to give change for to a stranger; she said she could get change at the grocer's just above - I then said I would give her change, and she purchased goods to the amount of 2l. 4s. or 2l. 5s.; she gave me the note, and I wrote on the back of it "Miss Turner, 14, Camden-street," which was the name and address she gave; when I gave her the change I said I would send a young man with her to see that all was right - she then said she was not going home, that she was going to the dress maker's, and the dress maker knew her - I told my young man privately to follow her, and not leave her till he had seen her home; my young man is not here - I afterwards found the prisoner was in custody - I had kept the note and given her the change; this is the note.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you sure it is the same note? A. Yes; it has my writing on it.
JAMES FOX (police-constable N 196). I received information that a 50l. note had been passed at Mr. Read's, by a young woman who had given a false address, and who had gone off with two young men - I followed, and took the prisoner in Chapel-street, about ten o'clock - I found these articles, which had been purchased at Mr. Read's, some on her person, and some at a dress maker's - I found on her four 5l. notes, one 10l. note, seventeen sovereigns, and 19s. in silver.
GEORGE DYER. I am a clerk in the Bank - this note was brought in on the 19th of July, and paid it in cash.
MARY HARWOOD. This is my note.
GEORGE READ. This is the note; I paid it away for goods to Bishop & Hall, in the Borough - I wrote the name on it in the prisoner's presence, and I took the number before I paid it away - I gave her 47l. 15s. in gold, notes, and silver; this property is what I sold her.
William Crossley, a tailor, at Carshalton, gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 17. - Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18330905-168]
1371. ELIZABETH WILLIAMS and SARAH EDWARDS were indicted for stealing, on the 30th of June, 1 pocket-book, value 6d.; 1 half-sovereign; 4 half-crowns; 7 shillings, and 1 sixpence, the monies of James Catchpool, from his person.
JAMES CATCHPOOL. I am a compositor, and work at Mr. Lewis', Finch-lane, Cornhill. On the 30th of June, I called in at the George and Dragon, in Hackney-road, about ten o'clock in the evening; I had a glass of sherry at the bar - while I was there, Edwards came in and asked if I would treat her with a glass of anything to drink, and on her renewing her application, I said she should have a glass of anything she liked; she had a glass of rum and shrub, and I paid for that and my sherry and came out, but I had not got more than twenty yards, when she came running after me, and asked if I was going home, I said, "Yes;" she then put her hand round my waist; I said, "You are going to rob me;" she said, "No I am not, upon my soul;" I said,"Go away; if you follow me I will give you in charge of the first policeman I meet;" I went on, she still followed me; I told her if she dared to follow me, I would give her in charge; she took no notice, but still followed me, and I struck her; I then met a friend of mine and told him of it - she then went away, and I went with my friend to another wine vaults and had a glass of sherry; while we were there, the two prisoners came in and Williams came up to me and said, "I don't think she meant to rob you;" I said, "She has not, and if she follows me I will give her in charge;" Williams then said,"I have not offended you, give me a glass;" I said I would not, but my friend said, "You had better give her a glass and have nothing more to do with her;" I then gave her a glass - Edwards then went away - I came out, Williams came after me, and asked me to give her a sixpence to get some gin; I gave her one and bade her good night; but as I was going down the street, she came after me again and said, "Give me another sixpence before I go;" she then put her arms round my waist, and I missed my money from my pocket; I then put my hand to my pocket and missed my pocket book - I said, "You have robbed me;" she said, "I have not, I have nothing but this sixpence;" the policeman was coming by, and I gave her in charge; she was taken to the watch-house - she still denied having anything, but when she was going to be searched, she said, she had 6s. or 7s.; I had lost a half-sovereign, which was in a small bit of paper, and four half-crowns and some silver, which I had taken in change of a sovereign; my money wasfound on her almost to a penny - Edwards was gone away at the time I was robbed.
Williams. Q. What time might it be when you saw me? Witness. A. I suppose about a quarter before twelve o'clock - I did not go to any public-house with you; I did not give you a sixpence to go and get some gin - you did not say you could not get it for that,
COURT. Q. You swear she said she had no money but sixpence? A. Yes, she did.
FERDINAND McKEE (police-sergeant H 4). I took the prisoner Williams - the prosecutor said he had been robbed of 18s. or 19s. in silver, and a half-sovereign in a bit of paper in his pocket-book; I took the prisoner to the station-house and found the money in her pocket; the pocket-book has not been found, but it was seen near the spot; the prosecutor had been drinking but was sober; Williams denied having any money but the sixpence; it was about a quarter past one o'clock on Sunday morning, the 30th of June.
Williams Q. Did I not tell you I had 14s. or 15s.? Witness. A. No, you denied having anything but the sixpence; but when you got to the station-house you said you had 7s. or 8s.; I did not hear you and the prosecutor talking about gin - I did not hear him speak of losing a crown piece.
Williams Defence. I was going home and met a gentleman who I had a child by - he gave me a sovereign; I then met a person about eleven o'clock, who had been very good to me when I had the cholera; I went to her place and treated her, and we had some rum and porter - I left her place about a quarter before one o'clock; I then met the prosecutor, he put his arm round my waist and asked me to go and have something to drink - we went to a public-house door, and he gave me sixpence and said, "Fetch half a pint of gin;" I said, "They will charge more for it at this late hour;" he then said he had no more money and he thought I had robbed him; but if I had robbed him of the silver, I should have had the pocket-book and the half-sovereign about me.
WILLIAMS - GUILTY. Aged 25.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
EDWARDS - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18330905-169]
1372. JOHN REECE was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of July, 1 hat, value 12s.; the goods of John Saunders; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
JOHN SAUNDERS. I am a pensioner on the East India Company. I have three children who live at the Bell and Crown, in Shoreditch - I go there two or three times a day, to see that they have proper necessaries, and supply them with money - I have another lodging for myself at the Lord Nelson, in St. Lukes - on the 26th of July, I went to the Bell and Crown to see the children, at eleven o'clock at night; I laid myself down and meant to sleep there; the landlord was tipsy and he came up and abused me for being there; he then went down and abused his servant, and said she should leave his house that day month, for I could not be in his house without her knowing it - I went down and begged him not to be angry with her; he took me by the collar and turned me out, and while he did so my hat fell off or was knocked off; two policemen came up to me just after, and asked if I had lost my hat; I said, "Yes," and they went and brought the prisoner with my hat - this is it.
THOMAS LINZAN (police-constable N 67). I was on duty in Kingsland-road, on the night in question - I saw the prisoner run down Crooked-Billet-yard, with a hat in his hand; I went on a few doors and saw the prosecutor; I asked, if he had lost his hat; he said, he had; I told him to rest still, and I went down the yard; I could not see the prisoner, but I saw the hat in a cart; I sat down by it a few minutes, the prisoner then came and took it; I called my brother-officer, and he was taken; he had taken the hat out of the cart, but before I could take hold of him, he stepped back, and put it into the cart again.
THOMAS MEADOWS (police-constable N 249). I went down the yard with the other officer, and he concealed himself there - I came up the yard and saw the prisoner going down; I followed him close; I heard the other officer say to him, "Do not put it down, it will do" - I then took the prisoner.
Prisoner. I live down that court and I was going home from work. Witness. No, he does not, we know him well.
CHARLOTTE CRABTREE. I produce a certificate of the prisoner's conviction, from Mr. Clark's office; I was a witness on that trial in this court - I know the prisoner is the man.
Prisoner's Defence. I had been that day to Blackheath, with a load of goods; I came home, had my supper, and went to the public-house; when I came out the prosecutor was fighting; I went home and went to bed; I was taken ill and had to come down, when the officer took me.
James Thomas Francis, a shoemaker, of Kingsland-road, gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 26. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18330905-170]
1373. JOHN JOHNSON was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of August, 1 silver spoon, value 14s.; the goods of Josiah Blackburn.
JAMES HENRY ARCHER. I am apprentice to a pawnbroker, at No. 8, Oxenden-street, Somers-town. On the 26th of August, the prisoner came there to pawn a silver spoon; he wanted 3s. on it - I asked him who sent it; he said, his father, who was a bricklayer - I detained the spoon, and told him to send his father; he did not come, and in about two hours' time, I went to see if his address was correct; it was not, and I took the spoon to the station-house; when I got home the prisoner was there, and said his father could not come; I took him into custody.
ALGERNON SMITH (police-sergeant S 22). I took the prisoner; he said, his mother had sent him to pawn this spoon, and she lodged in Brill-row, Somers-town; I went there, and saw her; she said, she had not sent him out since she sent him to work in the morning, and she had no silver spoon.
MARY ANN BLACKBURN. I am the wife of Josiah Blackburn; we live at No. 161/2, Clarendon-square; we employed the prisoner for about a fortnight, to go on errands; this spoon is one of ours, but I did not miss it till my attention was called to it.
GUILTY. Aged 11. - Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor - Whipped and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18330905-171]
1374. THOMAS COLEMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of July, 10 yards of velvet, value 3l., the goods of James Rozee.
JAMES ROZEE. I live in Great Pearl-street, and am a weaver. I had known the prisoner about five weeks; he worked in my shop - I was at work on this velvet, but on the 17th of July, I went out; I had eleven yards and a quarter in my loom - I left the prisoner there; he had velvet in his loom, the same as mine - I went out a little after ten o'clock, I returned a little after four; the prisoner was then gone, and his work and mine were both gone - the velvet in his loom was not mine, he had it from another master on his own account; I went directly and acquainted the two masters of it - Mr. Graham, of Spital-square is my employer - I had been that morning to Mrs. Simmons, to put up a loom for her - we have not heard of the velvet since; the prisoner did not return to my house; he was taken up the next day - my master gave me into custody as soon as I told him of the loss.
FRANCES SIMMONS. I live in Catherine-wheel-alley, Bishopsgate. On the 17th of July, the prosecutor came, and was security for a loom for me, and he put it up - he left me about four o'clock in the afternoon; he returned about six, and said, he had been robbed - I had not seen the prisoner for a week or two before that, but that night I saw him with his sisters at the Blue Ball public-house, in Cock-lane - I looked at him a long while, because he was clean, and I had not seen him so before; I asked his sisters, if he was not their brother; they said, no - I then looked at his hand, and saw one finger was off, and I knew it was him - I flew out of the house to look for a policeman, and the prisoner escaped - the next morning I went to Mr. Sturgeon's; he went with me to a house in Castle-street - we went up stairs, and Sturgeon persuaded the prisoner to come down; there was a poker taken up, but I do not know whether the prisoner took it, or whether it was a pipe in his mouth - he ran after me into the passage.
BENJAMIN LOCKWOOD. I went with her to the house; I stood at the door; she went up, and came down screaming murder; the prisoner came down, made use of bad expressions, and asked, what we wanted with him - he walked with Sturgeon and me to Church-street, Bethnalgreen; we met the police, and give him in charge.
MARY GHOST. I am the wife of Richard Ghost; I live under the room in which this velvet was; the prosecutor went out a little after ten o'clock on the 17th of July, and asked me to give an eye to the door; after he had been gone a little while, the prisoner came down; he went out, and came back again with a strange man, and they went up stairs together - the strange man came down soon after, and the prisoner followed him with a bundle under his right arm, and a bag in his left; he said to me, "Would you give an eye to the door?" I said,"Yes"; they then went away past my window; the prisoner came back again, and went up stairs, and when he came down again, he said, "I do not think my work safe here, and when this is done, I shall have no more;" I saw no more of him; I saw no one else go in.
WILLIAM BEDFORD (police-constable H 23). I took the prisoner, on the 18th of July - I searched him at the station-house; I found 9s. 6d. in silver on him, and 2d. in copper.
CHARLES LEFEVRE. I worked in Rozee's shop with the prisoner, on the 15th of July, from morning till night - he went out in the morning to get some tobacco; he was a long while gone, and when he came back, he said, he had been to his sister's to borrow 1d. to buy the tobacco; he then had his dinner, smoked his pipe, and worked till eight o'clock at night.
Prisoner's Defence (written). I had my work from Mr. Green, of Steward-street, which I took to Mr. Rozee, who engaged to learn me to make velvet; at length I found he was not a trust-worthy person, being in the habit of getting drunk for three or four weeks at a time; I heard that he had lived with a female for some years, until she was committed to Newgate for robbing the person's house who had engaged her to wash and char; he then sent the children to the workhouse, and left her till she was married to a Mr. Simmons: and now Mr. and Mrs. Simmons, Mr. Rozee, and the children all live in one room. On the day named in the indictment, a young man came to pay me some money, which he had borrowed at sundry times; we staid a short time; when we came down stairs I had my dirty linen, and the young man's dirty linen, to take to my sisters to wash; the door of the workshop has no fastening, and as I came down stairs, I said to Mrs. Ghost, "Be so good as to look after the door, as Rozee is out getting drunk again;" I then left the young man, and went home to my sister's to dinner. The street-door is always open, so that any person might go up or down the stairs without being perceived; and Mr. Rozee some time ago lost his work before, while he was out at the public-house.
George Hughes gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 30. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18330905-172]
1375. WILLIAM JOHNSON and WILLIAM THOMPSON were indicted for stealing, on the 24th of July, 1 watch, value 4l.; 1 watch-chain, value 6d.; and 1 seal, value 1d., the goods of John Burke.
ELIZABETH BURKE. I am the wife of John Burke, we live at No. 21, Turner's-buildings. I sell fruit - on the 24th of July, the prisoner and another boy came into my shop, I served the other boy with a halfpenny worth of apples - and while doing so, I had to step a little on one side, for a moment, and I saw one of the prisoners come round me, from the place where my husband's watch had hung which was over the mantel-piece in the shop; I looked and the watch was gone - the two prisoners ran off - the other boy went as far as the canal-bridge, and then he ran and got away - I called, "Stop thief," and the two prisoners were taken about one hundred yards off - the watch was found in the road, they ran.
JOHN NOBLE. I am a brush-maker. I live about half a mile from the prosecutor's house - I saw the two prisoners running and the prosecutor after them - I made a stop, the prisoners ran up to me, they said, "Do not stop us, it is for breaking a window" - I said they must stop, one of them tried to run but was taken directly.
ANN EDWARDS. I picked up this watch, in a row of houses, in the Shepherdess-fields - the prisoners had run past there - I took it to the station-house.
ISAAC GRUGEON (police-constable N 27). I was on duty on the evening of the 24th of July - I heardan alarm, and saw the two prisoners running - I pursued them - they were stopped by Noble, this watch was brought to the station-house.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Johnson's Defence. I was going down the street and three young lads ran past me - I then saw the prosecutor crying, "Stop thief," I had heard a window break just before, I ran and called, "Stop thief," and the witness stopped me.
Thompson's Defence. I was going down the street with Johnson, two lads ran past me and pushed me down, and then the witness stopped me - the prosecutor came up and said, she wanted a watch, but she did not think I was one of the boys.
JOHNSON - GUILTY. Aged 18.
THOMPSON - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Seven Years.

1377. JOHN PATTEN and JOSEPH LEYLAND were indicted for stealing, on the 29th of August, 1 watch, value 3l.; 1 seal, value 16s.; and 1 watch-key, value 6s., the goods of Thomas Seaman, from his person.
THOMAS SEAMAN. I am a letter-carrier, to the Two-penny Post-office, on the 29th of August, I was in Tabernacle-square, between nine and ten o'clock, I was intoxicated and did not know what I was about - I had a silver watch with me, with a gold key, and seal.
JAMES MIELL. I am acquainted with the prosecutor, I met him that night, he was drunk, there was a mob of forty or fifty people in Tabernacle-square, he was obstinate and would go into the mob - the two prisoners were there close to each other; they endeavoured to pick a quarrel with the prosecutor - I got him out of the mob, and on towards Old-street - he had his watch at that time, the two prisoners followed us, and Patten ran against the prosecutor's shoulder, and pulled the watch out of his pocket - the other prisoner was close to him - I took Patten by the collar - the policeman came up and I gave them both in charge - the watch has not been found, there was still some persons about - I positively swear I saw Patten take the watch.
Patten. How do you know the prosecutor had a watch? Witness. I saw it, and saw you draw it from him; I cannot tell what you did with it.
WILLIAM HOLLAND (police-constable N 146). About eleven o'clock at night, I heard of a robbery, and went out, and saw Miell with the prisoner Patten, I took him, and he proved to be the person I was looking after for another robbery, but the parties would not prosecute - Miell had been drinking a little.
WILLIAM MEDLICOTT. I am an officer. I took Leyland.
Patten's Defence. I had been on an errand to Chiswell-street, I was coming home through Tabernacle-square, and the witness charged me with this.
PATTEN - GUILTY. Aged 19.
LEYLAND - GUILTY. Aged 19.
Transported for Fourteen Years.

1379. JANE CORNWALL was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of August, 1 gown, value 5s.; 1 sheet, value 5s.; 1 tea-spoon, value 2s.; and 2 waistcoats, value 3s., the goods of Henry Rutter, her master - and 15 shirts, value 4l.; 4 waistcoats, value 10s.; 1 hat, value 15s.; 5 coats, value 3l.; 4 pair of trousers, value 1l.; and 1 book, value 1s., the goods of John Jones .
SARAH RUTTER. I am the wife of Henry Rutter. John Jones lives in our house, No. 19, Great Coram-street; the prisoner was my servant; she came on the 19th of September, in last year, and left me on the 31st of August last - I had given her a regular warning; after she was gone I missed the property stated - I sent an officer to the prisoner's lodging, who brought me a number of duplicates.
WILLIAM TOOLE (police-constable C 132). I took the prisoner - I went with her to the prosecutor - she there acknowledged the robbery, and took me to her lodging, and delivered me up sixty duplicates, among which are the duplicates of this property.
JAMES GILL. I am a pawnbroker. I have a great number of articles pawned by the prisoner at different times.
SAMUEL WILLIAMS. I am a pawnbroker. I have several shirts and coats pawned by the prisoner.

Prisoner. I throw myself on the mercy of the Court.

1380. HENRY THOMPSON was indicted for embezzlement.
CHARLES WESTLAKE. I am a butcher in Newport-market. The prisoner was my errand boy for about two months - he received money, on my account, every day, and was to return and give it to me.
ELEANOR SHEA. I am in the service of Mr. Hitchcock - I paid the prisoner on the 26th of August 2l. 16s. 6d. for his master; he put his name on this bill.
CHARLES WESTLAKE. He never paid this money to me - he was a very bad boy - I turned him off, and a week after I sent a man for this money, and found it had been paid.
JAMES WALSH (police-sergeant C 4). I took the prisoner - he said, he had received this money - and some money which I found on him, he said, was what he had received.
GUILTY. Aged 17. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18330905-178]
1381. JAMES CARTER was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of July, 1 spoon, value 2s., the goods of Caroline Stanley.
MARY SAUNDERS. I am servant to Caroline Stanley, she lives at Paddington. On the 16th of July, about three o'clock in the afternoon, I was coming down the kitchen stairs, and saw the prisoner go up the area steps, with the spoon in his hand, at his side - I ran after him and called, "Stop thief," he ran as far as he could to the Park-wall, and I saw him get over - he was caught in the Park, and was brought back with the spoon, which had been taken off the dinner-tray on the kitchen table - I know nothing of the prisoner.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me come down the area? A. No.
DONALD M'CLEAN (police-constable D 132). I was on duty, and a cab-man called to me, "There goes a thief;" I saw the prisoner running across the Park - I knew him, and followed him, crying, "Stop thief," a gentleman got out of a cab and stopped him, and I drew this spoon from under his waistcoat.
GUILTY. Aged 21. - Transported for Seven Years.

1383. SAMUEL DEAYTON was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of July, 7 sovereigns and 40 shillings, of Ann Welsh his mistress, in her dwelling-house.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
ANN WELSH. I live in the parish of St. Pancras, and keep a dairy - the prisoner was in my employ; on the 20th of July I was called out between twelve and two o'clock - I left Emily Probert and the prisoner there - I saw my cash-box when I went out - I had seen the contents of it safe the night before - it had then 36l. in it - when I returned home I missed seven sovereigns and forty shillings out of a little canvass bag - I had no one but the prisoner to milk my cows, and to carry out my milk; so I allowed him to do it - I afterwards had him taken - I had left my key in the cupboard where this cash box was.
COURT. Q. Is it your dwelling-house? A. Yes; there was no other person who could have taken the money - the prisoner had been eleven months with me.
Prisoner. I had bought you a load of hay, and you came out into the cow-shed for half an hour, the money might have been taken then. Witness. No, not that day; I did not.
EMILY PROBERT. I was left at home with the prisoner- he told me to go to the shed and look for a key; I did, and came back, and said, I could not find it; he told me to go and look into the further shed, where the grass was; I went, and returned, and said, I could not find it; it was the key of the school-room which my mistress had to let - when I went I left no one in the kitchen but the prisoner.
JAMES WELLS (police-constable S 30). I took the prisoner, he said, he knew nothing about this charge.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18330905-181]
1384. SAMUEL DEAYTON was again indicted for stealing, on the 9th of July, 1 coat, value 20s. and 1 waistcoat, value 8s. the goods of Ann Welsh, his mistress.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
ANN WELSH. I live in the parish of St. Pancras - three days after the loss of my money I missed a black coat and waistcoat, of my late husband's; they had been in the cupboard, in my kitchen, in which the money was deposited.
JAMES WELLS. I took the prisoner - I searched him, but found nothing; but I found, in a box, which he said was his, this duplicate, in a waistcoat pocket - it is for a coat and waistcoat, pawned in the name of Samuel Deayton, 46, Middlesex-street; the prisoner said, he knew nothing about them.
ROBERT MILTON. I am a pawnbroker. I have the coat and waistcoat, I cannot say who pawned them, but I gave the person this duplicate.
Prisoner's Defence. I know nothing about these things - I have been at the pawnbroker's, but it was to pawn my own property.
GUILTY. Aged 19. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18330905-182]
1385. SAMUEL DEAYTON was again indicted for stealing, on the 24th of April, 1 watch, value 30s. the goods of William Welsh.
WILLIAM WELSH. I am nephew of Ann Welsh. I lost the duplicate of a watch in April last - it had been pawned for 10s. at Mr. Griffiths.
JAMES ARCHER. I produce the duplicate to prove that this watch was pawned; and it was redeemed, to the best of my belief, by the prisoner - when he was taken this watch was found on him.
JAMES WELLS. I took the prisoner on the other charges - I found this watch on him; he was very desirous for the magistrate to allow him to have the watch again, but he would not - the prosecutor then claimed it; he described it to me before he saw it.
WILLIAM WALSH. This is my watch - the prisoner lodged with me in the month of March.
Prisoner. Q. Was your box broken open? A. No; but the duplicate was in it, and it was taken out.
GUILTY. Aged 19. - Transported for Seven Years longer.

[trialAccount t18330905-184]
1387. JOHN PINKS HOLLOWAY was indicted for feloniously, forging an acceptance on a bill of exchange, with intent to defraud Thomas Dring , against the statute, &c.
2nd COUNT for uttering the same.
3rd and 4th COUNTS stating it to be with intent to defraud Henry Usher.
THOMAS DRING. I am a ginger beer manufacturer, and live in Star-street, Edgeware-road. The prisoner took a public-house of me, the White Hart, Drury-lane; he took possession of it on the 8th of January; he took the fixtures and utensils, which came to 100l.; he paid me 30l. in cash, and two bills, one for 30l. at two months, and one for 40l. at four months; those bills were not honoured; and I promised to renew the 40l. for two months with a substantial acceptance, which he promised to get, and he enclosed this bill for 40l., on the 12th of May, in a letter, which was brought to me by Frederick Jervis- I had seen the prisoner write before, and I have every reason to believe it is his writing - I told Jervis the bill was not indorsed; it is accepted by Henry Usher.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. When did you first learn the bill was a forgery? A. I think on the 12th or 13th of May - I know that a bill of exchange is not negotiable without an indorsement - I put it into Mr. Williams's hands to try to get the money, but I received nothing for it.
Q. Long after you had ascertained that this was a forgery, did you not take from the prisoner a written agreement to return the public-house, and fixtures, and all the property you had sold him? A. No, I did not; Mr. Handley took it for me; he is a gentleman, who did live at the Hope, and now he lodges at my house - I know Mr. William Pinks, I don't know that he is rich, they told me he was godfather to the prisoner - I don't know when I went to Mr. Pinks to try to get the money, it might be a week afterwards.
Q. When you sent Mr. Handley to get re-possession, did you not engage to hold the prisoner harmless? A. I cannot charge my memory; I did not propose it; I caused it to be proposed, and he entered into a written agreement, but I never said it was because I thought I could get the money of old Pinks; I had received the 30l. of him in cash at first, but I should have been no great gainer if I had had the rent and taxes to pay.
Q. Did you not afterwards fly from that agreement, and say you would not do it? A. It was proposed to Mr. Usher, and I would not accept it, because I understood he had given a cognovit to pay so much a week to somebody.
Q. If you found out in May that the bill was a forgery, please to give the jury some honest reason why you put it off till September? A. He promised he would pay me if I would not proceed, I said if he would pay me the 40l. I would forgive him the 30l. - I went once to Mr. Pink with Usher, and Usher asked him if he would assist Holloway- I told him it was a forgery, and if he could not settle the bill, he must take the course of the law - I don't know that I told him I would prosecute Holloway, but I think I said if he would pay I would not prosecute.
Q. Did you not go to Holloway, after you had ascertained that the bill was a forgery, and after you had agreed to take back all the property, and sacked the 30l. which he had paid - did you not say that you could not make up your mind to do it, because you had lost your wife in the house? A. I might have done so, I would not swear that I did not; I don't recollect it; I have said so to other persons.
Q. Did you not afterwards propose that he should execute a bill of sale to Usher of all the property, and that Usher, whose name was forged, should befriend him in consequence of it? A. It was proposed in the presence of Usher and me, but I would not accept of it.
Q. Was not the condition that the man should deliver up all the property in the public house, to be followed by giving up the bill of exchange? and after having bound him by an agreement in writing, and led him to expect that you would deliver up the bill you are now prosecuting him for it? A. Certainly, I should be very happy to get the money.
Q. In the month of July did you go to the prisoner's wife in his absence, and ask her if old Pinks could be induced to make up this bill? A. I don't know that I used the word "Old Pinks," I might have said, Mr. Pinks, but I don't recollect it.
COURT. Q. Can you satisfy us, why you, who knew this bill was forged in May, should now appear to prosecute in September? A. From an impression that he would furnish means to take the bill out of my hands - if he had paid it, I would not have prosecuted him.
FREDERICK JERVIS. On the 12th of May, the prisoner asked me to take a letter to the prosecutor - I took it, the prosecutor opened it, and I saw a 40l. bill in it.
EDWARD HANDLEY. I took this bill of exchange to Mr. Usher about the middle of May - I called on the prisoner the next day, or the day after that, and told him it was a forgery, he acknowledged it was, but said, he was in hopes of providing for it when it became due.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. I live upon my means - I did keep the Hope Tavern - I took the agreement to the prisoner for him to sign it, to give up the house to Mr. Dring again - I will not swear whether it was Mr. Dring's writing or mine - I cannot call to mind whether the condition was that if the prisoner signed it the bill should be given up - I took it and I think the prisoner signed it, and I think it stated that if he gave up the house, the bill should be given up - I don't know what I did with the agreement - Mr. Dring would not agree to it.
HENRY USHER. I am a packer - I live at No. 29,Chicksand-street, Whitechapel. This acceptance is not my writing.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you been a tolerable large dealer in bills of exchange? A. No - I don't know whether I ever signed one in my life - I have not been in the habit of giving accommodation acceptances.
Q. Upon your solemn oath did you accept one for 26l. 10s., for the prisoner? A. I cannot say the sum, it was never uttered - it was destroyed in my presence by the prisoner - I never gave an acceptance in blank - I took a bill of sale of the prisoner, of all the things in the public-house, because I lent him 55l., and had to pay 30l., which I was bound for him to the distiller - I did not tell the prisoner if he would give me a bill of sale I would befriend him - I did not know, but that he had paid Mr. Dring - I took the bill of sale about the 26th or 27th of May - I have sold the property - I went to old Pinks with Mr. Dring to speak to him because I understood he was a friend to the prisoner; I did not ask Pinks for the money - I cannot recollect going to the prisoner's wife when he was not at home.
EDWARD HENRY BURRIDGE. I apprehended the prisoner on the 8th of August. NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18330905-185]
1388. WILLIAM SEARLE was indicted for feloniously forging on the 13th of April, a certain receipt for money with intent to defraud William Smith and Josiah Smith .
3 OTHER COUNTS, varying the manner of laying the charge.
JOSIAH SMITH. I am in parternership with William Smith, we are cabinet-maker s, and live in Red Lion-court, Charter-house-lane; the prisoner was in our employ as errand boy, for sixteen or seventeen months, we export goods from various wharfs.
FRANCIS SMITH. I am clerk to the prosecutor. I sent the prisoner in April to the New Leith and Berwick Wharf, with some packages to go to J. Morrison of Edinborough - I gave him some money, and he accounted to me for 2s. 10d. for the packages.
GEORGE MOSS. I am clerk at the New Leith and Berwick Wharf. On the 13th of April, I gave the receipt for 1s. 10d., it is now altered to 2s. 10d. - I don't know who I gave it to.
FRANCIS SMITH. This is the receipt, it is now 2s. 10d. - I believe the prisoner brought it to me - I cannot swear that positively.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Have you any recollection at all about it? A. It was his duty to give it, I cannot recollect who gave it to me.
NOT GUILTY.

1389. WILLIAM SEARLE was again indicted for forging on the 27th of April, a receipt for money, with intent to defraud William Smith, and Josiah Smith .
3 OTHER COUNTS, varying the manner of laying the charge.
JOSIAH SMITH. I am partner with William Smith, the prisoner was an errand boy, he was sent with goods to different wharfs, to be forwarded to our customers - I know nothing of this transaction.
WILLIAM EVANS Mc KENZIE. I am clerk at the New Leith and Berwick Wharf. On the 27th of April, a case was brought there - I gave this receipt for it - it was then 1s. 2d. but it has been altered to 2s. 2d. - I cannot say who brought it.
FRANCIS SMITH. I am clerk to the prosecutor. I have the receipt of the 27th of April, I believe the prisoner gave it to me, because there is his hand writing at the back, and the persons who bring these receipts do write their names on the back - I have the book here, it is entered 2s. 2d. but not to whom it was paid, there were two boys sent with the parcel.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How do you know that the other boy might not have made the alteration on this bill? A. Only by the prisoner's name being at the back.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18330905-187]
1390. WILLIAM SEARLE was again indicted for forging on the 6th of July a certain receipt for money, with intent to defraud William Smith, and Josiah Smith .
3 OTHER COUNTS, varying the manner of laying the charge.
JOSIAH SMITH. I am of the firm of William and Josiah Smith, the prisoner was our errand boy.
WILLIAM EVANS Mc KENZIE. On the 6th of July - I gave this receipt, but I cannot say to whom - it was for 1s. 6d. but has been altered to 2s. 6d.
FRANCIS SMITH. I can swear the prisoner delivered me this receipt with his own hands - I allowed him 2s. 6d. for it.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Have you been listening to the other two trials, and found that they went off, because the receipts could not be traced to the prisoner? A. Yes; I have been speaking to my master since the trial; I said "It was a pity we had not the boy here who went with the prisoner," he said, it did not matter- I believe there was a boy went with the prisoner on the 6th of July, the prisoner delivered this receipt to me, but I cannot say who made the alteration - I don't know whether the other boy is now in our service - I don't know who he was - we have many errand boys who come to our service, and leave it.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18330905-188]
1391. CHARLES SHEPHERD was indicted for stealing on the 3rd of September, at Hornsey, 1 silver cruet stand, value 4l. 10s.; 3 cruet stoppers, value 10s.; 1 spoon, value 4s.; and 1 mustard glass, value 2s. 6d., the goods of Joseph Hoare Bradshaw, in his dwelling-house.
WILLIAM TURTON. I am a groom and live at Hornsey, Mr. Bradshaw lives opposite to my master. On the 3rd of September, I saw the prisoner and another person about half-past one o'clock, in the day; the prisoner pulled off his shoes and stockings, and went into the door of Mr. Bradshaw's house - I waited about five minutes - I saw him come out and took him, with the property on him- I don't know what became of the other person.
JOSEPH HICKS. I am butler to Mr. Joseph Hoare Bradshaw, he lives there, it is his dwelling-house, and is in the parish of Hornsey - I did not see the prisoner till he was in the custody of the last witness - I went up andasked him what he had got, he said, "This," and opened his apron and showed me the cruet-stand - I said, "Is this all you have?" he said, "Yes," but I found this other property in his pocket, which I had put on the sideboard in the dining-room, twenty minutes before - this cruet-stand is worth 4l. 10s., the stopper 10s., the spoon 4s., and the glasses 2s. 6d., they are all my master's - I know nothing of the prisoner.
ROBERT CROUCH. I am an officer; I took the prisoner with this property on him.
Mr. Mitchell, a hair dresser, gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 20. - Transported for Life.

CHARLOTTE SANDELL. I am a bookseller. I was at my door near the prosecutor's; I saw a box sold to two ladies in a carriage - I don't know what was paid for it, but I saw some money.

1393. ELIZA JONES and MARTHA BENNETT were indicted for stealing on the 15th of June, 9 yards of linen, value 13s.; 3 pair of shoes, value 12s.; 1 comb, value 6d.; 5 skeins of twist, value 10d.; and 2 yards of galloon, value 2d., the goods of William Newman.
MARY NEWMAN. I am the wife of William Newman - he is a shoemaker, and lives at Uxbridge - Jones had lived servant with us, but had left us - I lost this property at different times; I could not tell when, but I was continually losing property while she was with me - the gentleman to whose service she went from me was not satisfied, he came to me and gave me some information - Bennett washed for me about three months ago; this property was found at Bennett's; Jones was afraid to take it home.
JOHN BIRCH. I am a constable of Uxbridge. I was employed to look after this property; I went to Jones, who gave me an apron, which she said she had taken from Mr. Newman, and said that was all she had in the house; but Mrs. Newman had been to Jones before me.
MARY NEWMAN re-examined. Q. Did you make Jones any promise? A. Yes; I told her, if she would tell me where she had taken my things I would forgive her, but she would not; I then took the officer with me - we found part of my property at Bennett's.
JOHN BIRCH re-examined. Q. What else did Jones say? A. She said she had taken a cut of Irish to make some aprons, and then another cut of Irish, to make three aprons, and three pairs of shoes - I found this piece of Irish at Bennett's and this pair of shoes - Bennett said this was one pair the girl gave her, that one pair she had worn out, and one pair she had lost; and this comb was also found.
MARY NEWMAN. The Irish has been washed, but I believe it to be mine - I can swear to this comb by a mark on it.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. I believe Jones' friends are respectable? A. Yes, truly so; and I believe this is her first crime.
JONES - GUILTY. Aged 16. Confined Three Months.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury and Prosecutor.
BENNETT - NOT GUILTY.

1394. JOHN LANGLEY CROWLEY was indicted for, feloniously altering a receipt for money, with intent to defraud William Frederick Jones and another. against the statute, &c.
2nd COUNT, for uttering the said altered receipt.
2 OTHER COUNTS, for forging a receipt, and uttering the same.
JOSEPH WILLIAM PERRY NEWSOM. I am clerk to Mr. William Frederick Jones and another; they are wharfinger s, at St. George's steam wharf - the prisoner was porter there; on the 28th of August I sent him out with two parcels, one was to Mr. Capron, and one for Mr. Wilson; I gave him this book, which is signed by the persons who receive the parcels and pay the money; I gave him two receipts for 1s. 6d. each, which was the charge for wharfage - these receipts in the book purport to be for 2s. 6d., he never paid them to me.
SAMUEL BLINKS. I live with George Capron, Esq. solicitor, in Saville-place - on the 28th of August the prisoner brought a parcel, I paid him 2s. 6d. for it; I signed the book; this is the receipt the prisoner gave me.
WILLIAM FREDERICK JONES. The money was never paid to me.
JOSEPH CLEMENTS. I took the prisoner.
GEORGE GARNER. The prisoner lodged with me - I sent the book to the wharf when the prisoner was apprehended.
GUILTY. Aged 32. - Transported for Seven Years.
1395. JOHN LANGLEY CROWLEY was again indicted for embezzlement.
WILLIAM FREDERICK JONES. The prisoner was our porter, and it was his duty to receive money; on the 28th of Augus t he received the money stated; the firm have never received it from him.
JOHN HILL. I am the proprietor of Hill's coffee-house, Charing-cross; on the 28th of August, the prisoner brought a horse to my house; I paid him 3l. 18s. 6d. for his employer, and he gave me a receipt, which I gave to the gentleman the horse was directed to.
JOSEPH WILLIAM PERRY NEWSOM. I gave the horse to the prisoner with this book and a receipt - I told him to take the horse to Charing-cross, and bring the book back - he took it, but never brought the money; thebook was sent some days afterwards - Richard Watson and others constitute the firm.

GUILTY. Aged 32.

1396. JOHN SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of September, 1 copper, value 10s. the goods of Elizabeth Lecornew, and fixed to a building, against the statute, &c.
JANE SALMON. I live with my mother; we had a house to take care of at Paddington; it belongs to Mrs. Elizabeth Lecornew, we live next door to it - on the 7th of September the prisoner came and asked to look at it, my mother gave me the key, and the prisoner and I went over the house; he said he liked it; he wished to speak to the landlady about it - he then went away, but returned again; he had the key, and went over the house again by himself, three times - there was a copper fixed in the wash-house; the prisoner brought the key back, but did not take the house - I afterwards went to show the house to a lady, and found the parlour shutters were open, which had before been shut, and this copper was in the parlour; this was the same day as the prisoner had been there - about eight o'clock in the evening I was walking outside with the child; a man came to the house, opened the parlour window, and took the copper out - I ran in, and said "Here is some man got the copper;" I ran after him, he threw down the copper at the corner of the Junction-road, and ran away out of my sight - I cannot swear it was the prisoner, but it was a man about his size, and he had a flannel jacket on - my mother had the care of the house - this is the copper,
GEORGE PETERS. I saw the prisoner go to the house, lift up the window, and take out the copper; he did not go in - he went up the town with it, and turned towards the Junction-road - I called to him "Master, put down that copper" - he put it down, and said "Here, my dear, I shall go." - he then ran off, I pursued him crying "Stop thief," the policeman and another man pursued him - the prisoner fell down and the policeman fell over him; the prisoner got up and ran again, but was taken - I never lost sight of him; I am sure he is the man.
GEORGE COMPTON (police-constable T 155). I heard the alarm and followed the prisoner; he fell down, and I over him; he got up and ran again, but I took him - he had a flannel jacket on.
Prisoner. It was not me with the copper.
GUILTY. Aged 36. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18330905-193]
1397. WILLIAM McDINE and JOHN DAVIS were indicted for stealing, on the 8th of September, 1 half-crown, the monies of John Hart, from his person.
JOHN HART. I am a labourer to Mr. Girling, bricklayer, Castle-street. On Sunday, the 8th of September, I was in Earl-street, Seven-dials, about two o'clock in the morning - I had been with some shop-mates and been drinking, but I had all my senses about me; I had not seen the prisoners before - but the prisoner McDine came up and challenged me to fight; he struck me in the eye and knocked me down - I had not said anything to him - he gave me a black eye; Davis then came as it were to assist me up, and slipped his hand into my right-hand breeches pocket where I had two half-crowns, and he took out one of them - I put my hand into my pocket and missed it - I challenged them with it, and enticed them up the street to give them something to drink, until I saw a policeman who took them; upon my solemn oath they were strangers to me, I had never seen them before to my knowledge.
McDine. Q. Can you solemnly swear that when I met you, you did not catch hold of my smock-frock and tear it before I struck you? Witness. A. No I did not.
Davis. Q. Did you not put 21/2d. into my hand, and tell me you would go and have some gin? Witness. A. No, I did not.
WILLIAM MACDONALD (police-constable T 36). I was on duty, and heard the two prisoners and the prosecutor coming up to my beat - the prosecutor asked them to give him his half-crown, or the first policeman he met he would give them into custody - I then went over and took them under a lamp; I found on Davis one half-crown piece, two sixpences, and fourpence-halfpenny in copper, all in the same pocket - I found nothing on the other prisoner; the prosecutor was not quite sober, but he knew perfectly well what he was about; I told him the nature of the charge, and that it was a very dangerous one, if he was not well aware of it - he said he was sure Davis was the person who took the half-crown from his pocket; the prosecutor had been drinking, but was perfectly sensible.
McDine. I was intoxicated, he tore my frock, this is it - I then struck him, and we both fell too fighting.
Davis. The money found on me, McDine gave me to take care of.
Mr. Fowler, of Silver-street, Golden-square, gave McDine a good character.
McDINE - GUILTY. Aged 22.
DAVIS - GUILTY. Aged 28.
Transported for Seven Years.
